## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 1 Results

The champion (score 23133.17) uses:
- State 0: Exploration (slow, rwm=5) with 3 transitions: go to state 1 if many neighbours (C=3, thresh=10), go to state 0 if blue perceived (C=7, clr=3), go to state 0 if few neighbours (C=4, thresh=5)
- State 1: Exploration (fast, rwm=72) with 1 transition: go to state 2 if black floor (C=0, p=0.9)
- State 2: ZigZag with 1 transition: go to... wait, n2x0 destination is 3 but there are only 3 states (0,1,2). This seems like a bug but it ran fine - perhaps destination 3 wraps or is invalid. Let me re-read: "D is indexed among the OTHER states only" - with 3 states, leaving state 2, others are [0,1], so D=0→state 0, D=1→state 1. D=3 would be... out of range. But it ran. Let me trust the result.

The second best (23183.37) uses GoAwayColor from green (clr=2 is green?) with repulsion. Actually color codes: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. GoAwayColor from green (color 2) makes sense - move away from green walls toward blue walls.

Key insight: Blue walls (color 3) border the rewarded upper strip. We need to:
1. Move toward blue (GoToColor, clr=3) or away from green (GoAwayColor, clr=2)
2. Once on black floor AND near blue walls, stay there

### Strategy for Round 2

1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Small edits to top 2-3 performers
3. **Exploration**: New topologies with strong blue-seeking + black-floor stopping

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Exploit #1: Tweak champion - change transition probabilities slightly
3. Exploit #2: Tweak champion - different rwm values
4. Exploit #2b: Based on #2 performer (GoAwayColor green + repulsion)
5. Exploit #3: Based on #3 performer (GoAwayColor + Stop + GoToColor blue)
6. New: 3-state: GoToBlue → Stop-if-black, with black+blue confirmation
7. New: 2-state: GoToBlue directly, stop when black
8. New: 3-state: Explore → GoToBlue → Stop
9. New: GoAwayGreen → ZigZag → Stop on black
10. New: 4-state with strong blue-seeking
11. New: Mix color perception and floor
12. New: GoToBlue + neighbor-based staying
13. New: Exploration + GoToColor blue + stop
14. New: Different topology

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 2 Results

The new champion (score 6344.75) is dramatically better than before. Its structure:
- **State 0**: GoToColor blue (clr=3) → if black floor (p=0.85) go to state 1, if sees blue (p=0.6) go to state 2
- **State 1**: Stop → if gray floor (p=0.25) go to state 0, if random (p=0.02) go to state 2, if sees green (p=0.4) go to state 0... wait let me re-read.

Actually re-reading state 1 (Stop): n1x0 destination=0→state 0, n1x1 destination=2→state 2 (but with 4 states, leaving state 1, others are [0,2,3], so D=0→s0, D=1→s2, D=2→s3). n1x2 destination=0→state 0.
- State 1 Stop: go to s0 if gray (p=0.25), go to s2 if random (p=0.02), go to s0 if sees green (p=0.4) → effectively stays stopped on black, escapes if gray
- **State 2**: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) → if sees blue (p=0.8) go to s0, if black floor (p=0.7) go to s1 (Stop)
- **State 3**: Exploration (rwm=10) → if sees blue (p=0.75) go to s0, if black floor (p=0.8) go to s1 (Stop)

The key pattern: GoToBlue → Stop on black. GoAway green redirects to GoToBlue. The logic is elegant.

The second best (6469.04) uses GoAway cyan (clr=6) → Stop → GoToBlue. Similar logic.

### Strategy
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Tweak probabilities, add transitions, adjust parameters
3. **Exploration**: Try variations - stronger stopping, different color combinations, adding neighbor-based transitions

Key observations:
- Black floor + blue perception = good stopping condition
- GoToBlue (clr=3) is the primary navigation
- GoAwayGreen (clr=2) or GoAwayCyan as backup
- Stop state needs strong retention (not leave when on black floor)
- State 1 (Stop) leaving to gray (p=0.25) allows re-navigation if pushed out

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 3

The champion (5886.34) is a 3-state machine:
- **State 0**: GoToBlue (clr=3) → Stop if black (p=0.85), Stop if sees blue (p=0.7), Stop if gray (p=0.4)
- **State 1**: Stop → GoToBlue if gray (p=0.2), Stop if random (p=0.02), GoToBlue if sees green (p=0.4)
- **State 2**: GoAwayGreen (clr=2) → GoToBlue if sees blue (p=0.8), Stop if black (p=0.75)

The top 5 all score ~5900-6000, clustering around the same structure. The pattern is clear:
1. GoToBlue navigates to blue walls
2. Stop on black floor (the target strip)
3. GoAwayGreen as a correction mechanism
4. Return to GoToBlue from Stop if gray (left the strip)

Key observations:
- The champion uses a **3-state** machine with state 0 having 3 transitions (including gray→stop which is useful for catching robots on gray returning to blue area)
- State 1 (Stop) has "sees green → go to GoToBlue" which helps robots on wrong black strip
- The Stop state escaping on gray with low probability (0.2) is crucial - too high and robots leave the good strip
- State 2 (GoAwayGreen) as a redirector works well

### Strategy
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Fine-tune probabilities around champion's values, try adding a 4th state with another helper
3. **Exploration**: Try different combinations - maybe a FlockCenter state to keep robots together, different stop conditions, adding neighbor-based transitions to stop state

Let me focus on:
- Tweaking transition probabilities by ~10%
- Adding/removing transitions from the stop state
- Trying 4-state extensions of champion
- Some genuinely new topologies

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 4

The champion (5249.00) is a 3-state machine with a key improvement: State 1 (Stop) now has **4 transitions** including `--n1x3 0 --c1x3 0 --p1x3 0.05` (escape if black floor with p=0.05). This small probability of escaping from black is interesting - it might help robots that are stuck on the wrong (lower) black strip.

The structure that consistently works:
- **State 0**: GoToBlue (clr=3), 3 transitions: stop if black (p=0.85), stop if sees blue (p=0.65), stop if gray (p=0.45)
- **State 1**: Stop, 4 transitions: go to GoToBlue if gray (p=0.2), random escape (p=0.02), go to GoToBlue if sees green (p=0.4), go to GoToBlue if black (p=0.05)
- **State 2**: GoAwayGreen (clr=2), 2 transitions: go to GoToBlue if sees blue (p=0.8), go to Stop if black (p=0.75)

The 4th transition on Stop (escape on black with low p=0.05) seems to help escape the wrong strip. The "sees green → GoToBlue" transition also helps robots on the lower strip redirect.

Key insight: The escape from black floor (p=0.05) is a low probability that allows robots on the wrong strip to eventually re-navigate. Too low (0.01) might not help enough; too high and robots leave the good strip.

### Strategy for Round 5

1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: 
   - Tweak p0x2 (gray→stop threshold) around 0.45
   - Tweak p1x3 (black escape) around 0.05 - try 0.03, 0.07, 0.1
   - Tweak p0x1 (blue→stop) around 0.65
   - Add more transitions to state 2
3. **Exploration**:
   - Try adding a 4th state that handles the "wrong strip" case more explicitly
   - Try GoAwayGreen in state 0 instead of GoToBlue
   - Try different state 1 structures

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 5

The champion (5201.81) has converged to a clear structure:
- **State 0**: GoToBlue (clr=3), 3 transitions: stop if black (p=0.85), stop if sees blue (p=0.65), stop if gray (p=0.45)
- **State 1**: Stop, 4 transitions: go to s0 if gray (p=0.2), random escape (p=0.02), go to s0 if sees green (p=0.4), go to s0 if black (p=0.05) 
- **State 2**: GoAwayGreen (clr=2), 2 transitions: go to s0 if sees blue (p=0.85), go to s1 if black (p=0.8)

The best performers are all very similar. The key difference between #1 (5201) and #2 (5222) is p2x0=0.85 vs 0.80 and p2x1=0.8 vs 0.75 in state 2. Higher probabilities in state 2 seem better.

Observations:
- Adding a 3rd transition to state 2 was worst performer #2 - avoid
- Routing state 1's random escape to state 2 (GoAwayGreen) instead of state 0 was worst performer #1 - avoid
- 4-state with exploration state 3 (5322) is decent but not as good as 3-state

The structure is nearly converged. I need to:
1. Fine-tune probabilities around current champion
2. Try some variations: different gray thresholds, different blue detection probabilities
3. Try adding a 4th transition to state 0
4. Explore whether state 1 can escape via GoAwayGreen (state 2) instead of GoToBlue (state 0)

Key parameters to vary:
- p0x0 (black→stop): 0.80-0.90
- p0x1 (blue→stop): 0.60-0.70
- p0x2 (gray→stop): 0.40-0.55
- p1x0 (gray→navigate): 0.15-0.25
- p1x2 (green→navigate): 0.35-0.50
- p1x3 (black→navigate): 0.03-0.08
- p2x0 (blue→s0): 0.80-0.90
- p2x1 (black→s1): 0.75-0.85

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 7

The champion hasn't improved this round (still 5133.67). The best performers this round all scored ~5229-5260, worse than the champion. This suggests we're near a local optimum.

Key findings from failures:
- p0x0=0.86 is worse than 0.85 - keep at 0.85
- p1x1=0.015 is worse than 0.02 - keep at 0.02
- p1x0=0.18 and p1x2=0.42 are worse - keep at 0.20 and 0.40
- p0x2=0.43 and 0.47 and 0.48 are all worse than 0.45

The current champion parameters seem quite well-tuned. The marginal improvements are very small now. I need to try:
1. Keep champion
2. Fine-tune very small changes around champion values
3. Try some genuinely different structures to escape the local optimum

The champion's "good" values:
- p0x0=0.85, p0x1=0.65, p0x2=0.45
- p1x0=0.2, p1x1=0.02, p1x2=0.4, p1x3=0.04
- p2x0=0.87, p2x1=0.82

Things NOT yet tried:
- p1x3=0.02 or 0.025 (even lower escape from black)
- p2x1=0.84, 0.80 
- p0x2=0.44, 0.46 (very fine-grained)
- State 2 with 3 transitions adding a gray condition
- Adding a 4th state that handles robots on wrong strip via explicit GoAwayGreen → GoToBlue pipeline
- Trying p1x2 (green→navigate) at 0.35 or 0.45

Also, we haven't tried routing state 1's black-floor escape to state 2 (GoAwayGreen) instead of state 0 (GoToBlue) - this might help robots on the wrong strip navigate via GoAwayGreen first.

Let me also try some genuinely different approaches:
- A 2-state machine: GoToBlue + Stop with multiple transitions
- Using cyan walls (if any) or different color combos

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 8

Champion still at 5133.67 - no improvement. The search is clearly stuck in a tight local optimum. 

Key findings:
- p0x1=0.66 is worse, p0x1=0.64 is slightly worse → 0.65 is optimal
- p2x0=0.86 is worse → 0.87 is better than 0.86
- n1x3 destination=1 (→GoAwayGreen when on black) is much worse (5564) → the escape should go to state 0 (GoToBlue), not state 2
- p0x2=0.44 is slightly worse than 0.45
- p1x0=0.21 is slightly worse than 0.2

The champion parameters seem tightly optimal. Let me think about what hasn't been tried:
- p2x1=0.80 was tried (5226) - slightly worse
- p1x3=0.025 - not reported yet this round, could try again
- Combining p0x2=0.44 with p1x0=0.21

Since we're very near the local optimum and fine-tuning is barely moving the needle, I should dedicate more machines to **genuine exploration** of different topologies that might find a different basin:

1. Try using GoToBlue as the stopping trigger differently
2. Try a 2-state machine with more transitions  
3. Try replacing GoAwayGreen with something else in state 2
4. Try having state 0 start as GoAwayGreen and state 2 as GoToBlue
5. Try a 4-state machine with a dedicated "wrong strip escape" state
6. Try adding a neighbor-count condition to Stop state (C=4 few neighbors → re-explore)

The key insight I haven't exploited: robots on the **lower** black strip see green walls but NOT blue walls. So in Stop state, I could use "sees blue" as a STAY condition (high p) and "sees green" as a LEAVE condition. Currently the Stop state uses "sees green → GoToBlue" which is the right direction but maybe the probability needs tuning, or using "NOT sees blue" (C=4 condition on color perception isn't available, but C=7 with green can substitute).

Let me also try: what if state 1's black-floor escape uses a very low p like 0.02 (matching the random escape) - or what if we eliminate it entirely?

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 9 - Final Round

This is the last round. The champion is still 5133.67 and hasn't improved in several rounds.

Key findings from round 9:
- p2x0=0.89 (5163.77) is the best new result - slightly better than 0.87 but not beating champion
- State 1 with 3 transitions (no black escape) is much worse (6001) - confirms the 4-transition stop state is important
- Replacing state 1's random escape with "sees blue → stay" (5860) is very bad
- GoAwayGreen as starting state (8145) is very bad

The champion's exact parameters appear locally optimal. The score hasn't moved in 3 rounds.

For the final round, I should:
1. Keep champion (mandatory)
2. Try combinations of the best-found parameters: p2x0=0.89 (from best this round) combined with other tweaks
3. Try p2x0=0.88, 0.90, 0.91 to narrow down the optimal value
4. Try very small tweaks around the champion's other parameters
5. Try a few genuinely different structures in case there's a better basin

The most promising lead: p2x0=0.89 gave 5163.77 (close to champion). Try p2x0=0.89 combined with p2x1=0.83 or 0.84.

Also: The 4-state machine with the green-perception escape in stop state (rank 3, 5222) is interesting - try tweaking it.

Final strategy: maximize exploitation of the champion topology while including a few diverse candidates.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. At every simulation step the loop function adds +1 to the score for each robot that is NOT inside the target region. The score is MINIMISED, so a lower score means the swarm reached the region earlier and more robots stayed in it.

THE TARGET REGION is the upper strip of the arena. It is NOT identified by a unique floor colour: the floor is BLACK in BOTH the upper and the lower strip, and GRAY in between. Only the UPPER black strip is rewarded. Sitting on the lower black strip scores exactly as badly as standing in the middle of the arena.

HOW TO TELL THE TWO STRIPS APART. At the start of the run the arena walls light up: the two walls bordering the REWARDED upper strip are BLUE, the two walls bordering the penalised lower strip are GREEN. Colour is the only reliable cue.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. A black-floor condition is useful to detect that a strip has been reached, but on its own it sends half the swarm to the wrong strip. It must be combined with colour-driven motion - move toward BLUE, or move away from GREEN - to reach the correct one. Once inside, robots should stay rather than keep exploring, since every step spent outside costs a point. Clustering tightly is NOT rewarded: only being inside counts.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 6 walls, inner radius 0.86 m. 27 e-puck robots start scattered uniformly in a square of half-width 0.84 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.77 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
